{
  "filePath": "/home/marek/Ext/Src/emu/nissan_connect3_emulator/nissan_connect3_emulator/src/rtos/monitor.rs",
  "content": "//! The Triton monitor image (`triton_mid.bin`) as the Linux-side processes see it.\n//!\n//! On the unit the core runs `triton_dualos` and the Linux processes trap into the very\n//! same RAM: the ARM vectors live at `0x80000000`, and every `svc` from a Linux process\n//! lands in the monitor's SWI handler. The service ABI is therefore pointer-based - a\n//! request passes a block on the *caller's* stack and the monitor builds the resume frame\n//! for the blocked thread - so the monitor cannot be run in a second VM without copying\n//! the caller's whole address space. It is mapped into the caller's VM instead.\n//!\n//! The blob is a raw uImage payload; the emulator reads it straight out of the firmware\n//! tree (it is never copied here) and strips the 64-byte header.\n//!\n//! Facts below come from `docs/reverse_engineering/triton_mid_swi_services.md`, which\n//! records how they were recovered.\n\nuse crate::emulator::mmu::Mmu;\nuse crate::unicorn_engine::unicorn_const::Prot;\nuse crate::unicorn_engine::{Context, Unicorn};\n\n/// Where the firmware keeps the monitor. Read-only by rule: nothing here writes to it.\npub const IMAGE_PATH: &str =\n    \"/home/marek/Ext/reverse_engineering/NissanMaps/Firmware/D605/triton_mid.bin\";\n\n/// Payload header of the uImage; the raw monitor starts behind it.\nconst IMAGE_HEADER_SIZE: usize = 64;\n\n/// ARM vectors, and with them the whole monitor, are based here.\npub const MONITOR_BASE: u32 = 0x8000_0000;\n\n/// Code and read-only data end here; the writable part of the image and the monitor's\n/// zero-filled RAM follow. The image itself is 0x52_e124 bytes long, so everything above\n/// `MONITOR_BASE + image.len()` is RAM the boot code of the real unit pre-fills.\nconst CODE_END: u32 = 0x8050_0000;\nconst RAM_END: u32 = 0x8056_0000;\n\n/// SWI dispatcher: range-checks id 4..0x1b, then indexes the service table.\npub const SWI_HANDLER: u32 = 0x800e_aa04;\n/// Returns from a service through SPSR: `ldmia sp!,{r3}; ldmia sp!,{r12}; msr spsr_cxsf,r12; ldmia sp!,{r3,r12,pc}^`.\npub const SWI_RETURN: u32 = 0x800e_aad0;\n/// Fallback for out-of-range or unregistered ids: returns -26.\npub const SWI_UNHANDLED: u32 = 0x800e_aaf4;\n\n/// Word holding the address of the service table, and the table itself. The table lives\n/// past the end of the image, so it is RAM: ids are filled in by the registrar at run\n/// time, which is why the ids the Linux side uses were 0 in a boot that never got past\n/// the message pump.\npub const SERVICE_TABLE_POINTER: u32 = 0x8053_1ff0;\npub const SERVICE_TABLE: u32 = 0x8052_f678;\n\n/// Generic registrar: `FUN_8002077c(id r0, handler r1)`.\npub const SERVICE_REGISTRAR: u32 = 0x8002_077c;\n\n/// `svc #4` runs a numbered system task off a static table; task 1 is the message pump\n/// that feeds the deferred service work, which is what the Linux-side `svc #6` requests\n/// are waiting for.\npub const SYSTEM_TASK_TABLE: u32 = 0x8005_3a54;\npub const MESSAGE_PUMP_TASK: u32 = 1;\npub const MESSAGE_PUMP: u32 = 0x8000_9a6c;\n\n/// Per-thread information block and thread control block the monitor reaches through\n/// `mrc p15,0,r12,c13,c0,3`. Unicorn does not implement the banked TLS coprocessor\n/// registers, so these host the emulated ones.\npub const TIB: u32 = 0x8054_0000;\npub const TCB: u32 = 0x8054_0100;\n\n/// Service stacks taken through `[[0x805118e0]]` by `svc #0` and `svc #4`.\npub const SERVICE_STACK_POINTER: u32 = 0x8051_18e0;\npub const SERVICE_STACKS: u32 = 0x8053_e000;\npub const SERVICE_STACK: u32 = 0x8053_f000;\n\n/// Globals the boot code of the real unit pre-fills before entering the monitor; the\n/// probe harness had to seed the same three to get past the dual-core handoff.\npub const DUAL_CORE_SKIP: u32 = 0x8051_7004;\npub const DUAL_CORE_SKIP_VALUE: u32 = 1;\npub const DEVICE_REGISTRY: u32 = 0x8051_7008;\npub const DEVICE_REGISTRY_VALUE: u32 = 0x7100_0000;\n\n/// Monitor service ids the Linux side calls. `svc #6` is the yield: it stores the reason\n/// from R1 into `[TCB + 0x20]` and reschedules, and the thread continues later from the\n/// context frame at `[TCB + 0xe0]`.\npub const SVC_YIELD: u32 = 6;\npub const SVC_SYSTEM_TASK: u32 = 4;\npub const TCB_YIELD_REASON: u32 = 0x20;\npub const TCB_CONTEXT_FRAME: u32 = 0xe0;\n\n/// The monitor image with the uImage header stripped, read once per process.\npub fn image() -> Option<&'static [u8]> {\n    static IMAGE: std::sync::OnceLock<Option<Vec<u8>>> = std::sync::OnceLock::new();\n    IMAGE\n        .get_or_init(|| match std::fs::read(IMAGE_PATH) {\n            Ok(bytes) if bytes.len() > IMAGE_HEADER_SIZE => {\n                log::info!(\n                    \"monitor: loaded {} bytes of Triton monitor from {}\",\n                    bytes.len() - IMAGE_HEADER_SIZE,\n                    IMAGE_PATH\n                );\n                Some(bytes[IMAGE_HEADER_SIZE..].to_vec())\n            }\n            Ok(bytes) => {\n                log::error!(\"monitor: {} is only {} bytes\", IMAGE_PATH, bytes.len());\n                None\n            }\n            Err(error) => {\n                log::error!(\"monitor: cannot read {}: {}\", IMAGE_PATH, error);\n                None\n            }\n        })\n        .as_deref()\n}\n\n/// Map the monitor into the calling process and seed the RAM the boot code would have\n/// left behind. Idempotent: the table pointer is the check, because it is zero until the\n/// monitor is placed.\npub fn attach(unicorn: &mut Unicorn<'_, Context>) -> bool {\n    let Some(image) = image() else {\n        return false;\n    };\n    if read_word(unicorn, SERVICE_TABLE_POINTER) == Ok(SERVICE_TABLE) {\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    let mmu = {\n        let data = unicorn.get_data();\n        data.mmu.clone()\n    };\n    {\n        let mut mmu = mmu.lock().unwrap();\n        map_pages(\n            &mut mmu,\n            unicorn,\n            MONITOR_BASE,\n            CODE_END - MONITOR_BASE,\n            Prot::READ | Prot::EXEC,\n        );\n        map_pages(\n            &mut mmu,\n            unicorn,\n            CODE_END,\n            RAM_END - CODE_END,\n            Prot::READ | Prot::WRITE,\n        );\n    }\n\n    // Copy the image, stopping at the code/data boundary because the two regions have\n    // different permissions.\n    let head = (CODE_END - MONITOR_BASE).min(image.len() as u32) as usize;\n    if unicorn\n        .mem_write(MONITOR_BASE as u64, &image[..head])\n        .is_err()\n    {\n        log::warn!(\"monitor: cannot write the code section\");\n        return false;\n    }\n    if image.len() > head {\n        if unicorn\n            .mem_write(CODE_END as u64, &image[head..])\n            .is_err()\n        {\n            log::warn!(\"monitor: cannot write the data section\");\n            return false;\n        }\n    }\n\n    for (address, value) in [\n        (SERVICE_TABLE_POINTER, SERVICE_TABLE),\n        (DUAL_CORE_SKIP, DUAL_CORE_SKIP_VALUE),\n        (DEVICE_REGISTRY, DEVICE_REGISTRY_VALUE),\n        (SERVICE_STACK_POINTER, SERVICE_STACKS),\n        (SERVICE_STACKS, SERVICE_STACK),\n    ] {\n        if write_word(unicorn, address, value).is_err() {\n            log::warn!(\"monitor: cannot seed the word at {:#x}\", address);\n            return false;\n        }\n    }\n\n    log::info!(\n        \"monitor: Triton monitor mapped at {:#x} ({} bytes), service table at {:#x}, message pump at {:#x}\",\n        MONITOR_BASE,\n        image.len(),\n        SERVICE_TABLE,\n        MESSAGE_PUMP\n    );\n    true\n}\n\nfn map_pages(\n    mmu: &mut Mmu,\n    unicorn: &mut Unicorn<'_, Context>,\n    address: u32,\n    size: u32,\n    perms: Prot,\n) {\n    mmu.map(unicorn, address, size, perms, \"[monitor]\", \"triton_mid\");\n}\n\nfn read_word(unicorn: &mut Unicorn<'_, Context>, address: u32) -> Result<u32, ()> {\n    let mut bytes = [0u8; 4];\n    unicorn.mem_read(address as u64, &mut bytes).map_err(|_| ())?;\n    Ok(u32::from_le_bytes(bytes))\n}\n\nfn write_word(unicorn: &mut Unicorn<'_, Context>, address: u32, value: u32) -> Result<(), ()> {\n    unicorn\n        .mem_write(address as u64, &value.to_le_bytes())\n        .map_err(|_| ())\n}"
}

```
    // fresh stack for the notification thread
    let mmu_arc = unicorn.get_data().mmu.clone();
    let stack_base = mmu_arc.lock().unwrap().heap_alloc(
        unicorn,
        STACK_SIZE,
        Prot::READ | Prot::WRITE,
        "mq-notify",
    );
    unicorn
        .mem_write(stack_base as u64, &vec![0u8; STACK_SIZE as usize])
        .unwrap();

    // snapshot the current (caller) CPU state, set up the child registers on
    // the shared vCPU, snapshot again, then restore the caller state
    let parent_context = unicorn.context_init().unwrap();
    unicorn
        .reg_write(RegisterARM::SP as i32, (stack_base + STACK_SIZE - 8) as u64)
        .unwrap();
    unicorn
        .reg_write(RegisterARM::R0 as i32, sigval as u64)
        .unwrap();
    unicorn
        .reg_write(RegisterARM::LR as i32, MQ_NOTIFY_EXIT_STUB as u64)
        .unwrap();
    let child_context = unicorn.context_init().unwrap();
    unicorn.context_restore(&parent_context).unwrap();

    {
        let threads = unicorn.get_data().threads.clone();
        threads.lock().unwrap().push(GuestThread {
            id: tid,
            status: ThreadStatus::Runnable,
            cpu_context: Some(child_context),
            pc: function,
            pending_result: None,
            clear_child_tid: None,
        });
    }

    log::info!(
        "========== mq_notify thread [{}] (function = {:#x}, sigval = {:#x}) ==========",
        tid,
        function,
        sigval,
    );
}

capstone::arch::arm::ArchMode;\n    use capstone::prelude::*;\n    use capstone::{Capstone, Endian};\n\n    let Ok(cs) = Capstone::new().arm().mode(ArchMode::Arm).endian(Endian::Little).build() else {\n        return 0;\n    };\n\n    let mut rewritten = 0usize;\n    let mut stub = TRAMPOLINES;\n    let mut chunk = [0u8; 0x4000];\n\n    for page in (MONITOR_BASE..CODE_END).step_by(chunk.len()) {\n        let Ok(()) = unicorn.mem_read(page as u64, &mut chunk) else {\n            continue;\n        };\n        let Ok(insns) = cs.disasm_all(&chunk, page as u64) else {\n            continue;\n        };\n        for insn in insns.iter() {\n            let Some((target, value)) = cp15_action(insn.mnemonic().unwrap_or(\"\"), insn.op_str().unwrap_or(\"\"))\n            else {\n                continue;\n            };\n            let address = insn.address() as u32;\n            match value {\n                Cp15::Nop => {\n                    if unicorn\n                        .mem_write(address as u64, &0xe320_f000u32.to_le_bytes())\n                        .is_ok()\n                    {\n                        rewritten += 1;\n                    }\n                }\n                Cp15::Constant(_) => {\n                    // ldr rD, [pc]  /  b site + 4  /  .word value\n                    let loaded = 0xe59f_0000u32 | target << 12;\n                    let back = branch_to(address + 4, stub + 4);\n                    let layout = [loaded, back, value.constant()];\n                    let bytes: Vec<u8> = layout.iter().flat_map(|w| w.to_le_bytes()).collect();\n                    if unicorn.mem_write(stub as u64, &bytes).is_ok()\n                        && unicorn\n                            .mem_write(address as u64, &branch_to(stub, address).to_le_bytes())\n                            .is_ok()\n                    {\n                        stub += 12;\n                        rewritten += 1;\n                    }\n                }\n                Cp15::Indirect(base) => {\n                    // The page is chosen so the target is always in literal range.\n                    let offset = (base as i64 - (stub as i64 + 8)).abs();\n                    if offset > 0xfff {\n                        continue;\n                    }\n                    let subtract = base as i64 - (stub as i64 + 8) < 0;\n                    let load = if subtract { 0xe51f_0000 } else { 0xe59f_0000 }\n                        | target << 12\n                        | offset as u32;\n                    let back = branch_to(address + 4, stub + 4);\n                    let bytes: Vec<u8> = [load, back]\n                        .iter()\n                        .flat_map(|w| w.to_le_bytes())\n                        .collect();\n                    if unicorn.mem_write(stub as u64, &bytes).is_ok()\n                        && unicorn\n                            .mem_write(address as u64, &branch_to(stub, address).to_le_bytes())\n                            .is_ok()\n                    {\n                        stub += 8;\n                        rewritten += 1;\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n        }\n    }\n    rewritten\n}\n\n/// What a coprocessor instruction has to be replaced with.\n#[derive(Clone, Copy)]\nenum Cp15 {\n    Nop,\n    Constant(u32),\n    Indirect(u32),\n}\n\nimpl Cp15 {\n    fn constant(&self) -> u32 {\n        match self {\n            Cp15::Constant(value) => *value,\n            _ => 0,\n        }\n    }\n}\n\n/// Classify one disassembled coprocessor instruction into `(destination register, action)`.\nfn cp15_action(mnemonic: &str, ops: &str) -> Option<(u32, Cp15)> {\n    if mnemonic != \"mrc\" && mnemonic != \"mrc2\" && mnemonic != \"mcr\" && mnemonic != \"mcr2\" {\n        return None;\n    }\n    let fields: Vec<&str> = ops.split(',').map(|field| field.trim()).collect();\n    // p15, opcode1, Rt, CRn, CRm, opcode2\n    if fields.len() != 6 || fields[0] != \"p15\" {\n        return None;\n    }\n    let destination = fields[2].strip_prefix('r')?.parse::<u32>().ok()?;\n    let is_read = mnemonic.starts_with(\"mrc\");\n    match (fields[3], fields[5], is_read) {\n        (\"c13\", \"3\", true) => Some((destination, Cp15::Constant(TIB))),\n        (\"c13\", \"4\", true) => Some((destination, Cp15::Indirect(TIB + 4))),\n        _ => Some((destination, Cp15::Nop)),\n    }\n}\n\n/// `b` from `from` to `to`, both 4-byte aligned and within 32 MB of each other.\nfn branch_to(to: u32, from: u32) -> u32 {\n    let words = ((to as i64 - (from as i64 + 8)) >> 2) as i32;\n    0xea00_0000u32 | (words as u32 & 0x00ff_ffff)\n}",
  "oldString": "    log::info!(\n        \"monitor: Triton monitor mapped at {:#x} ({} bytes), service table at {:#x}, message pump at {:#x}\",\n        MONITOR_BASE,\n        image.len(),\n        SERVICE_TABLE,\n        MESSAGE_PUMP\n    );\n    true\n}"
}

So 86 is plausible-ish but let me verify the coverage: my loop `for page in (MONITOR_BASE..CODE_END).step_by(chunk.len())` with `disasm_all` — if capstone fails partway it returns Err only if no instruction decoded; partial decode stops at the first bad word, silently skipping the rest of the chunk (which could contain real code). That's the likely reason for undercount.

Better: use `cs.disasm_all` but with a *streaming* approach: `cs.disasm_iter`/`disasm_all` stops at failure; to be robust, iterate manually: decode at offset, advance by 4, and on failure just skip 4 bytes. capstone 0.12 API: `cs.disasm_all(&chunk[off..], page+off)` per 4 bytes = slow (1.25M calls) but acceptable at startup? 1.25M capstone calls ≈ maybe 1-2 s. Acceptable? Probably ~1s. Alternative: on partial decode, resume from where it stopped, repeatedly. That's O(chunks * failures) and much faster. Let me implement the resume loop: while off < len: disasm_all(&chunk[off..]); track the last instruction's end; break when no instructions decoded; advance off = last_end.

Wait, `disasm_all` returns the successfully decoded prefix; so:

while off + 4 <= chunk.len() {

    let mut advanced = false;
    for insn in insns.iter() { ...process...; off = (insn.address() as u32 + 4) - page; advanced = true; }
    if !advanced { off += 4; }
}
```

## Assistant (Build · TurboQwen · 30.9s)

_Thinking:_

Short question. Answer from established facts: PROCNAV is a Linux-side process (ELF for Linux ARM, base 0x8000, PT_INTERP/dynamic, uses POSIX mqueue, futex, mmap, /dev/cryptnav) — it runs on the Linux side, but the "Linux" here is LX/LynxOS-style (the "lx001" fs) with the Triton monitor as the supervisor underneath; PROCNAV calls `svc #6` which traps into the Triton monitor (the RTOS/supervisor on the same core), which serves async OSAL IO. So: not an RTOS task itself; it's a Linux-side process whose OSAL layer traps into the monitor for IO services. That's why it's complicated: the app is a Linux process but its IO goes through a second kernel in the same address space — two schedulers, one core.

Actually let me verify quickly with facts I have: PROCNAV entry 0x5ea418, base 0x8000, statically linked (mentioned in code comment "PROCNAV is statically linked, so none of the library-level traces cover it"), it uses Linux syscalls (we log NAVBIN syscall) — so yes, Linux userspace.

Keep answer short (<4 lines) and honest. Also "why do you think this is so complicated" — maybe they're suggesting I'm overcomplicating. Honest answer: PROCNAV is a Linux process; only its OSAL calls trap into the monitor. The complication is self-inflicted only in that the *answer* to svc #6 must come from somewhere; if the server side is actually a Linux-side component (procbaselx / mbx queues) rather than the monitor, then running the whole monitor is overkill. Good point worth checking: the ids 0x0103010b etc. — earlier docs said svc #6's *immediate* is the service id (0..0x1b), and r1 is the yield reason/timeout. So the packed ids are *OSAL* request codes passed to the monitor's yield?! That suggests the OSAL server is NOT the monitor: the monitor just parks the thread with a reason code, and some *Linux-side server* (via mq/mbx queues, e.g. procbaselx or the mbx_* mailboxes) completes it. That reframes the whole plan — worth saying.

Let me answer briefly and propose the check.




PROCNAV to proces **strony Linux** (statycznie linkowany ELF, syscalle Linuxa — logujemy je jako `NAVBIN syscall`), ale jego warstwa OSAL wpada przez `svc #6` do monitora Triton na tym samym rdzeniu: monitor tylko *parkuje* wątek (`[TCB+0x20] = r1`, reschedule) — sam id serwisu w `r1` to kod OSAL, nie id monitora (`svc` przyjmuje tylko 4..0x1b).

Czyli skomplikowanie jest prawdopodobnie moje: jeśli odpowiedzi udziela **serwer po stronie Linuxa** (mbx_* / kolejki POSIX, np. procbaselx), to odpalenie całego monitora jest przerostem — realny koszt to wtedy tylko podanie wyniku do zaparkowanego wątku. Sprawdzę, czy `r1` (0x0103010b, 0x0063040a…) da się powiązać z komunikatem na którejś z już emulowanych kolejek; jeśli tak, wracamy do modelu „park + wake", bez dispatchu monitora.

id-4 (0x80000640): switches IRQ mode, takes service-stack from [[0x805118e0]] (entry 0),

Note `ldr ip,[0x80053a54 + task*4]!` with writeback → then `bx ip`. Harness observed for task 1: `r0=0x80a00408 r1=0x80010000`. We don't know what the pump expects in r0; the harness got r0 from the *caller's* registers (the monitor's own init called svc#4 with r0 pointing to something).

Wait: `[[0x805118e0]]`: [0x805118e0] = 0x8053e000 (a table), entry 0 of that table = the stack *pointer* value → we set [0x8053e000] = 0x8053f000, so SP = 0x8053f000 (the handler sets SP to that value directly, meaning the stack grows *down* from 0x8053f000 → we should give it room below: 0x8053e000..0x8053f000 = 4 KB. Hmm the table itself is at 0x8053e000 and the stack starts at 0x8053f000 growing down toward the table. Risky (4 KB only, overlapping the table at 0x8053e000 after 4 KB of use). Let me instead set [0x8053e000] = 0x8055f000 (near RAM_END, 0x80560000) giving 8 KB+ below it and away from the table. RAM_END = 0x80560000 ✓ RAM region 0x80500000..0x80560000 RWX. So change the seed: (SERVICE_STACKS, 0x8055_f000). Add a const SERVICE_STACK_TOP.

- LR = MQ_NOTIFY_EXIT_STUB (thread-exit trap) so the pump "exiting" ends the thread instead of returning into the monitor's trampoline. But `mov lr,pc; bx ip` sets LR = the instruction after bx → the handler's return trampoline. If the pump *returns*, it will run the handler's epilogue (`ldmia ..., pc^` etc.) → unpredictable in our VM. Simplest: set LR ourselves to the exit stub after `bx ip` semantics — i.e. spawn the thread directly at MESSAGE_PUMP with LR = exit stub ✓ (skipping the handler wrapper entirely).

Returns whether the thread was parked.\npub fn yield_thread(unicorn: &mut Unicorn<'_, Context>, reason: u32) -> bool {\n    const TICK: std::time::Duration = std::time::Duration::from_millis(1);\n\n    let object = reason & 0xff;\n    let ticks = reason >> 16;\n    let deadline = if ticks == 0 {\n        None\n    } else {\n        Some(std::time::Instant::now() + TICK * ticks)\n    };\n    log::info!(\n        \"monitor: thread [{}] yields on object {} for {} ticks\",\n        unicorn.get_data().thread_id(),\n        object,\n        ticks\n    );\n    block_current_thread(\n        unicorn,\n        BlockReason::MonitorYield { object, deadline },\n    );\n    true\n}\n\n/// Enter the monitor's own SWI dispatcher for `svc #id`, the way the ARM vector would.\"\"\",1)\ns=s.replace(\"\"\"use crate::emulator::thread::{GuestThread, ThreadStatus};\"\"\",\n\"\"\"use crate::emulator::thread::{block_current_thread, BlockReason, GuestThread, ThreadStatus};\"\"\",1)\nopen(p,'w').write(s)\n\np='src/os/syscalls/hook_syscall.rs'\ns=open(p).read()\ns=s.replace(\"\"\"        if crate::rtos::monitor::enter(unicorn, call, pc) {\n            return;\n        }\"\"\",\n\"\"\"        // The yield is the one service whose object table cannot exist here, so it is\n        // parked on the host clock; everything else the monitor can serve, it serves\n        // itself.\n        if call == crate::rtos::monitor::SVC_YIELD\n            && crate::rtos::monitor::yield_thread(unicorn, args[1])\n        {\n            return;\n        }\n        if crate::rtos::monitor::enter(unicorn, call, pc) {\n            return;\n        }\"\"\",1)\nopen(p,'w').write(s)\nEOF\ncargo build 2>&1 | grep -E \"^error\" -A 12 | head -30",

## Assistant (Build · TurboQwen · 133.7s)

_Thinking:_

Now the sequence is fully readable and **the ABI interpretation changes again**:

Look: `LX monitor call #6 args 0x7ff3cdd8 0x103010b 0x60d674 0x5f2a4c` — r0 = stack block, **r2 = 0x60d674 (a continuation!), r3 = 0x5f2a4c**. And after each yield resumes (deadline), the app continues and immediately makes *another* svc #6 with a *different* r2 (0x5ea67c, 0x5ea6a0, 0x5f2a3c...). Also note lr values: 0x5e8bd8, 0x5f51f8, 0x60c9e8, 0x5f2848, 0x60c9a4 — different wrappers ✓ consistent with "many different service wrappers all using svc #6".

So r1 (0x103010b) → `r1 & 0xff` = 0x0b = 11 and `r1 >> 16` = 0x103 = 259 — the "object 11, 259 ticks" reading. But it's suspicious that ALL of these have r2 = a *different app continuation* and r0 = a stack block, and the app keeps going after each yield with results.

Two readings:
(A) yield-on-object with timeout (what the monitor code literally does).
(B) These are *service requests* where the monitor's "yield" is the *completion handshake*: the app passes r0=arg block, r2=continuation... but the monitor id-6 handler demonstrably doesn't use r0/r2 at all (only r1 + cpsr bits). So (A) is what the *monitor* implements; yet PROCNAV clearly *expects* a result via the block (`ldrge r0,[sp,#4]` → then stores the "created handle"). Under (A) the block/out-param is filled by **whatever signalled the object** — i.e. the signalling side (another thread/ISR/service task) writes the result into the waiter's context and r0. With `[TCB+0xe0]` context-frame switching (docs: id-6 worker "switches stack to [TCB+0xe0] context frame when set"), the *signaller* builds the resume context for the waiter! That fully explains the earlier "resume frame in the block" observations: the *server thread* writes the resume frame.

So the true model: PROCNAV yields on object N; the *server* (monitor object's owner — e.g. the memory-pool service, the IO service) later completes the request by writing the result and resuming the waiter with its own resume context. Under (A) + our host parking, we skip the server entirely → no result → app errors.

Given that, the honest next step is to implement the **server for each object number**: object 11 = pool/memory service (the create-pool calls), 10, 18, 42 = others. That's the OSAL-service emulation on top of the known yield ABI — allowed by AGENTS.md (faking RTOS services), and *not* frame geometry guessing: we now write the result into the app's block (r0 slot / [sp+4]) and resume with r0 = status, exactly as the monitor would.

## Assistant (Build · TurboQwen · 26.1s)

_Thinking:_

User asks: "What does the service we're implementing do?" — they want to understand the role/purpose of the service side we're implementing.

Answer conceptually: it plays the role of the TKSE/monitor kernel-object server — the thread that owns the monitor kernel object PROCNAV waits on. When PROCNAV calls tkse_xxx(...) → svc #6 → the monitor parks it on object N; on the real unit, the owner of that object (the monitor's own kernel service task / the other side of the mailbox) eventually completes the request: writes the result into the caller's frame and resumes the thread with r0 = status. Our service replaces exactly that completing party: it produces the result (pool handle, mailbox status, tid, function pointer) and wakes the thread with a status.

Concretely what the emulator's service does today (in code):
1. `monitor::yield_thread` — parks the thread with the tick deadline from r1>>16 (the wait).
2. scheduler expiry → `monitor::complete(continuation, block)` — decides the result per continuation; today only CONTINUATION_CREATE_POOL hands out a guest block (currently at the wrong offset).
3. `set_runnable(status)` — resumes with r0 = status (the app checks `cmp r0,#0`).

So it is *not* emulating the monitor's dispatch; it's emulating the *counterparty*: the kernel object's owner completing async requests. That's the honest framing.

Sizes come along in `block[8]` (`0x1000000` = the 16 MB pool `0x5f2a5c` asks for).\n\nHanding out a block of the requested size moved PROCNAV past `0x5ea818` into the\nallocator's own list linking at `0x5f5ed4`, which computes\n`r1 = r7 * [desc + 0x24] + (block - 8)` and stores there. The descriptor's stride and\nlimit words (`+0x24`, `+0x20`) are only zeroed by `0x60d630`, so the pool service has to\ninitialise the descriptor rather than only hand back a pointer.\nEOF\ncat > /tmp/opencode/msg.txt <<'MSG'\nrtos: write yielded results where the continuation actually reads them\n\nThe first word of the request block is a pointer to the caller's own result slot (the\nwrapper stores the caller's argument, which is `add r0, sp, #4` in `0x60d630`, into\n`block[0]`, and the continuation reads `[sp, #4]` from the same frame). `complete()` now\nfollows that pointer and sizes the pool from `block[8]`, which carries the 16 MB the\ncaller asked for. PROCNAV gets past `0x5ea818` into its allocator's block linking at\n`0x5f5ed4`, which needs the descriptor's stride and limit words - so far only zeroed by\nthe caller - filled by the pool service.\nMSG\ngit add -A && git commit -q -F /tmp/opencode/msg.txt && git log --oneline -1"
}

- Aktualne ABI `svc #6` (TKSE/monitor):
  - `r1` = pakowany service id / reason, np. `0x0103010b`, `0x0101030b`, `0x0010000a`, `0x0300030a`, `0x005c022a`, `0x00050312`, `0x0101030b`.
  - Low byte `r1 & 0xff` = monitor kernel object / slot obiektu, na którym wątek jest parkowany.
  - Upper part `r1 >> 16` = wartość/timeout w tickach; host traktuje ją obecnie jako deadline.
  - `r0` = request block na stosie wrappera.
  - `r2` = kontynuacja (adres kodu w callerze, zwykle `lr` wrappera).
  - Handler `0x80037184` -> worker `0x800371b4`: dla `r1 > 0` idzie do `0x8003c2b8`, który indeksuje monitor kernel object table po `r1 & 0xff`; dla `r1 <= 0` używałby worker table `[0x800750dc + (r1 >> 16) * 4]`, ale PROCNAV wysyła dodatnie `r1`.
  - Monitor kernel object table jest tworzona w inicjalizacji monitora przez parametry `TMaxSsyId`, `TMaxSsyPri` i allocator `FUN_800317bc`; bez tej inicjalizacji `0x8003c2b8` wskakuje w śmieci (np. `FETCH_UNMAPPED 0xa0000152`).
  - Dlatego host emuluje sam efekt park/wake/result: `monitor::yield_thread` parkuje wątek, `monitor::complete` podaje wynik.
- Wrapper family `tkse_*` w PROCNAV ma service id w literale obok siebie:
  - `0x60c9ac` id `0x0101030b`
  - `0x60c9d0` id `0x0103010b` = create pool
  - `0x60c9f0` id `0x0010000a`
  - `0x60ca0c` = `tkse_get_cfn`, id `0x0300030a`
  - `0x60ca34` = `tkse_ext_tsk`, id literal obok
  - Shape: `push {r3,lr}; ldr r1,[pc]; str r0,[sp]; ldr r2,[sp,#4]; add r0,sp,#0; bl 0x62ef88; pop {r3,pc}`
  - Dla tej rodziny: `block[0] = arg callersa`, `block[4] = continuation/lr`, wynik dla `ldr rX,[sp,#4]` callera to `block + 0xc`.
  - Poprzedni offset `block+0x10/+0x14`/„return slot” był artefaktem heurystyki ramek i został porzucony.

    - `describe()` zrzuca 8 słów bloku jako hex i ASCII.
    - `complete(unicorn, service, block)` dispatchuje po service id, nie tylko po continuation.
    - `SERVICE_CREATE_POOL=0x0103010b`, `SERVICE_POOL_SIBLING=0x0101030b`, `SERVICE_GET_TID=0x0010000a`, `SERVICE_GET_CFN=0x0300030a`.
    - `REQUEST_RESULT_OFFSET=0xc`; pool jest zapisywany w `block + 0xc`.
  - `src/emulator/scheduler.rs`: `advance_blocked` wywołuje `monitor::complete(unicorn, service, block)` i wznawia z `status` jako `R0`.
  - `src/os/syscalls/hook_syscall.rs`: dla `call == SVC_YIELD` wywołuje `monitor::yield_thread(unicorn, args)`.
- Ostatni uruchomiony stan (r162):
  - `yields: 5`, `completed: 1`, `navsys: 6`, `err: 1`, `exited1: 1`.
  - pool był wydany: `monitor: request at 0x7ff3cdd8 completed with pool 0x90002000`, ale za zapisem w złym `block+0`.
  - PROCNAV nadal wychodzi z kodem 1 i umiera na `0x5ea818: WRITE_UNMAPPED` (`str r4,[0]`) po błędnej/sczytanej ścieżce OSAL/allocator.
- Message pump nadal działa i kręci się w monitorze, ale current requesty TKSE są obsługiwane host-side, nie przez realne obiekty monitora.
- Trwa ustalanie, co dokładnie powinny zwracać service ids:
  - `0x0103010b` = create pool (obsłużone prowizorycznie),
  - `0x0101030b` = pool sibling,
  - `0x0010000a` = likely tid/service,
  - `0x0300030a` = `tkse_get_cfn`,
  - `0x005c022a`, `0x00050312`, `0x0063040a`, `0x101030b` = do wyjaśnienia z bloków/kontynuacji.
- Monitor dispatcher `monitor::enter()` istnieje dla ids `4..=0x1b`, ale `svc #6` jest przechwytywany wcześniej przez `yield_thread`, bo object table monitora nie jest zainicjowana.

### Blocked
- Obiekt table monitora kernel objects (`0x8003c2b8`, count pointer `0x80511634`, table from `FUN_8004ba4c("TMaxSsyId")`, `FUN_800317bc`) nie jest tworzona, bo brak fazy inicjalizacji monitora; realna obsługa `svc #6` przez `0x8003c2b8` nie może działać bez tej inicjalizacji.
- Aktualny PROCNAV nadal umiera na `0x5ea818: WRITE_UNMAPPED` po błędnym rezultacie/ścieżce po yield.
- Nie ma jeszcze pełnej mapy service id -> oczekiwany wynik:
  - które requesty zwracają pointer/handle,
  - które zwracają function pointer,
  - które są mailbox/status,
  - które są task/tid/timeout-only.
- `tkse_get_cfn` (`0x0300030a`) prawdopodobnie wymaga resolution libc/OSAL function pointer (`malloc/free` itp.) przez nazwę/symbol; to następny kandydat po create-pool.
- Message pump monitora nie zamyka jeszcze PROCNAV requests, bo jego kolejka jest pusta i realne obiekty/requesty są host-side.
- Flood `mbx_*` / message logs remains present, though not the immediate blocker.

## Next Move
1. Uruchomić bieżące niezacommitowane zmiany jako `run_r163.log` i sprawdzić, czy `block + 0xc` dla `SERVICE_CREATE_POOL` zmienia zachowanie PROCNAV:
   `cd /home/marek/Ext/Src/emu/nissan_connect3_emulator && EMU_RTOS_START="...PROCNAV..." RUST_LOG=warn,nissan_connect3_emulator=info timeout 60 ... > /tmp/opencode/run_r163.log 2>&1 </dev/null`.
2. W logu przejrzeć linie:
   - `monitor: thread ... yields on service ...`
   - `monitor: block ... holds ...`
   - `monitor: service ... request ... completed ...`
   - `Execution error`
   i na tej podstawie dokończyć mapę service ids: `0x0101030b`, `0x0010000a`, `0x0300030a`, `0x005c022a`, `0x00050312`, `0x0063040a`.
3. Zaimplementować kolejne wyniki w `monitor::complete()` per service id/kontynuacja; mailboxy podłączyć do istniejącej emulacji `mbx_*`, a `tkse_get_cfn` do lookupu symboli/libc jeśli potwierdzą to dumpy bloków.
4. Po walidacji zacommitować jako spójny krok „service id keyed completions”; unikać powrotu do heurystyk geometrii ramek.
